Um, yeah—so, what finally scared me into making the appointment is work stuff. I’m a camera operator/editor, and I keep making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.

Yeah, I do. I mean, it’s not like I’m trying to sit there and obsess over it, but those mistakes stick in my head because they’re so… basic. Um… I’m not sure. Not in a “specific way” exactly? Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.

It’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.

So… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.

It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”

That’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. Not in a “specific way” exactly? Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these mistakes later, are you only thinking about what happened, or are you also worried about making mistakes in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. I’m a camera operator/editor, and I keep making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike I’ll label camera cards wrong, or miss a timecode note, or export a client cut with the wrong lower-third still in it even after I swear I checked it. And the thing is, these aren’t complicated things—like, I train other people on this kind of workflow sometimes. It’s embarrassing. I’ll be driving home and suddenly think, “Wait, did I fix that graphic?” and then it ruins my whole evening. So… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these mistakes after they happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, it’s not like I’m trying to sit there and obsess over it, but those mistakes stick in my head because they’re so… basic. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or sending out an export and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I thought I checked it.\n\nAnd then I’ll replay it, like, “How did I miss that?” It’s embarrassing because I know that workflow. I’ve done it a million times. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about it later when I’m doing something totally unrelated, like making dinner, and then I’m annoyed all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to try to prevent these mistakes from happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I try to. That’s kind of the whole problem—I’ll make these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make, like labeling camera cards wrong or missing a timecode note, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need a better system.”\n\nSo I’ll check the export again, or look at the lower-thirds again, because I’ve sent out a client cut with the wrong one still in it even after I thought I checked. Which is… ugh, it’s embarrassing. And then I start making little notes to myself, or trying to be extra careful, but sometimes I get sidetracked by another part of the workflow. Like someone asks me about a shot list or whatever, and suddenly I’m not even sure if I actually checked the thing or just thought about checking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform certain actions in a specific way to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Not in a “specific way” exactly? Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these mistakes later, are you only thinking about what happened, or are you also worried about making mistakes in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. And then the next project I’m worried I’m going to miss something like that again, so it’s not just the one mistake, it’s this feeling like I can’t totally trust myself with the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns outside of your work as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work? I don’t know. I’m trying to think.\n\nI mean, I definitely think about work stuff the most, because that’s where the mistakes are kind of… visible, you know? Like there’s a client or a deadline or other people see it. Outside of that, I’m not sure I’d say I repeatedly think about mistakes in the same way.\n\nLike, I’ll worry if I forgot something normal, I guess—sorry, I’m drifting—like I’ll leave a store and think, “Did I get everything?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the same loop as the work mistakes. So… I haven’t really noticed it as much outside of work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or sending out an export and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I thought I checked it.\n\nAnd then I’ll replay it, like, “How did I miss that?” It’s embarrassing because I know that workflow. I’ve done it a million times. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about it later when I’m doing something totally unrelated, like making dinner, and then I’m annoyed all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to try to prevent these mistakes from happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I try to. Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.

The thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m like, how did I miss that? I do this for a living.

For fun stuff… I don’t know, if I’m editing something personal or taking photos, I might get irritated if I do something sloppy, but it doesn’t hit the same. The work mistakes are the ones that stick because there are other people relying on it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F43.0, F48.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so, what finally scared me into making the appointment is work stuff. I’m a camera operator/editor, and I keep making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike I’ll label camera cards wrong, or miss a timecode note, or export a client cut with the wrong lower-third still in it even after I swear I checked it. And the thing is, these aren’t complicated things—like, I train other people on this kind of workflow sometimes. It’s embarrassing. I’ll be driving home and suddenly think, “Wait, did I fix that graphic?” and then it ruins my whole evening. So… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these mistakes after they happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, it’s not like I’m trying to sit there and obsess over it, but those mistakes stick in my head because they’re so… basic. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or sending out an export and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I thought I checked it.\n\nAnd then I’ll replay it, like, “How did I miss that?” It’s embarrassing because I know that workflow. I’ve done it a million times. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about it later when I’m doing something totally unrelated, like making dinner, and then I’m annoyed all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to try to prevent these mistakes from happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I try to. That’s kind of the whole problem—I’ll make these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make, like labeling camera cards wrong or missing a timecode note, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need a better system.”\n\nSo I’ll check the export again, or look at the lower-thirds again, because I’ve sent out a client cut with the wrong one still in it even after I thought I checked. Which is… ugh, it’s embarrassing. And then I start making little notes to myself, or trying to be extra careful, but sometimes I get sidetracked by another part of the workflow. Like someone asks me about a shot list or whatever, and suddenly I’m not even sure if I actually checked the thing or just thought about checking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform certain actions in a specific way to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Not in a “specific way” exactly? Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these mistakes later, are you only thinking about what happened, or are you also worried about making mistakes in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. And then the next project I’m worried I’m going to miss something like that again, so it’s not just the one mistake, it’s this feeling like I can’t totally trust myself with the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns outside of your work as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work? It doesn’t feel like the same loop as the work mistakes. So… I haven’t really noticed it as much outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns when it comes to your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, at least.\n\nLike with relationships, I’ll sometimes wonder if I said something awkward, but then I get distracted by—like, I was texting my sister the other day and then remembered I never paid that parking thing, and then I’m looking for the notice, and then I forgot what I was even worried about. Sorry, that’s not really answering it.\n\nDaily life stuff, I guess I have normal “did I forget something?” thoughts, but I don’t feel like I’m stuck replaying personal mistakes over and over the way I do with work. Sorry, that’s not really answering it.\n\nDaily life stuff, I guess I have normal “did I forget something?” thoughts, but I don’t feel like I’m stuck replaying personal mistakes over and over the way I do with work. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas outside of work where you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can think of. I mean, I get bothered by random stuff, like if the apartment’s a mess or I forgot to call someone back, and then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m making coffee and then somehow I’m checking the weather and—sorry.\n\nBut repeatedly thinking about one thing outside of work? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the work mistakes do. It’s more like little things pop up and then I’m onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever bothered by thoughts about mistakes or concerns outside of work that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something I can’t stop thinking about. I mean, I’ll have little worries pop up, like “Did I forget to respond to that message?” or “Did I leave laundry in the washer?”—actually I do that a lot, leave it sitting there and then it smells weird, which is so annoying—but those thoughts don’t usually stick in my head for hours or anything.\n\nOutside of work it feels more scattered, I guess. Something bothers me, then something else grabs my attention, and I’m on to that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed being stuck on mistakes or concerns outside work in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are the thoughts about mistakes that bother you mostly about work, or do they also happen with things you do for fun or in your free time?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.\n\nThe thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m like, how did I miss that? I do this for a living.\n\nFor fun stuff… I don’t know, if I’m editing something personal or taking photos, I might get irritated if I do something sloppy, but it doesn’t hit the same. The work mistakes are the ones that stick because there are other people relying on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble stopping thoughts about mistakes when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say trouble stopping them, no. Like if I’m doing something I enjoy, I might notice I messed something up and get irritated for a minute—like, “ugh, that was dumb”—but then I usually end up distracted by the thing itself or some other detail.\n\nI mean, if I’m editing something just for me, I can get picky, but it doesn’t feel like those work mistakes where it keeps sitting in my chest afterward. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about it later when I’m doing something totally unrelated, like making dinner, and then I’m annoyed all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to try to prevent these mistakes from happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I try to. That’s kind of the whole problem—I’ll make these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make, like labeling camera cards wrong or missing a timecode note, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need a better system.”\n\nSo I’ll check the export again, or look at the lower-thirds again, because I’ve sent out a client cut with the wrong one still in it even after I thought I checked. Which is… ugh, it’s embarrassing. And then I start making little notes to myself, or trying to be extra careful, but sometimes I get sidetracked by another part of the workflow. Like someone asks me about a shot list or whatever, and suddenly I’m not even sure if I actually checked the thing or just thought about checking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform certain actions in a specific way to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Not in a “specific way” exactly? Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these mistakes later, are you only thinking about what happened, or are you also worried about making mistakes in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. And then the next project I’m worried I’m going to miss something like that again, so it’s not just the one mistake, it’s this feeling like I can’t totally trust myself with the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns outside of your work as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work? I don’t know. I’m trying to think.\n\nI mean, I definitely think about work stuff the most, because that’s where the mistakes are kind of… visible, you know? Like there’s a client or a deadline or other people see it. Outside of that, I’m not sure I’d say I repeatedly think about mistakes in the same way.\n\nLike, I’ll worry if I forgot something normal, I guess—sorry, I’m drifting—like I’ll leave a store and think, “Did I get everything?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the same loop as the work mistakes. So… I haven’t really noticed it as much outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns when it comes to your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, at least.\n\nLike with relationships, I’ll sometimes wonder if I said something awkward, but then I get distracted by—like, I was texting my sister the other day and then remembered I never paid that parking thing, and then I’m looking for the notice, and then I forgot what I was even worried about. Sorry, that’s not really answering it.\n\nDaily life stuff, I guess I have normal “did I forget something?” thoughts, but I don’t feel like I’m stuck replaying personal mistakes over and over the way I do with work. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas outside of work where you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can think of. I mean, I get bothered by random stuff, like if the apartment’s a mess or I forgot to call someone back, and then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m making coffee and then somehow I’m checking the weather and—sorry.\n\nBut repeatedly thinking about one thing outside of work? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the work mistakes do. It’s more like little things pop up and then I’m onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever bothered by thoughts about mistakes or concerns outside of work that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something I can’t stop thinking about. I mean, I’ll have little worries pop up, like “Did I forget to respond to that message?” or “Did I leave laundry in the washer?”—actually I do that a lot, leave it sitting there and then it smells weird, which is so annoying—but those thoughts don’t usually stick in my head for hours or anything.\n\nOutside of work it feels more scattered, I guess. Something bothers me, then something else grabs my attention, and I’m on to that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed being stuck on mistakes or concerns outside work in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are the thoughts about mistakes that bother you mostly about work, or do they also happen with things you do for fun or in your free time?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.\n\nThe thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m like, how did I miss that? I do this for a living.\n\nFor fun stuff… I don’t know, if I’m editing something personal or taking photos, I might get irritated if I do something sloppy, but it doesn’t hit the same. The work mistakes are the ones that stick because there are other people relying on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble stopping thoughts about mistakes when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say trouble stopping them, no. Like if I’m doing something I enjoy, I might notice I messed something up and get irritated for a minute—like, “ugh, that was dumb”—but then I usually end up distracted by the thing itself or some other detail.\n\nI mean, if I’m editing something just for me, I can get picky, but it doesn’t feel like those work mistakes where it keeps sitting in my chest afterward. With fun stuff it’s more like… annoyed, then I move on. Or I start fixing something else and forget what I was mad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thinking about mistakes at work is affecting your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I mean, I’m sure if I come home annoyed from work I’m probably not the most fun person to be around, but I can’t say, like, “yes, this is ruining my relationships” or anything.\n\nDaily life outside work… maybe it just follows me home a little sometimes? But then I’ll get caught up in dinner or a show or, like, realize I forgot to buy paper towels again and now that becomes the thing. So I’m not sure it’s the mistake-thinking specifically affecting everything outside work. I notice it most when I’m still in work mode, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “I’m avoiding this because I might mess it up” kind of way.

Not in a “specific way” exactly? Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these mistakes later, are you only thinking about what happened, or are you also worried about making mistakes in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. And then the next project I’m worried I’m going to miss something like that again, so it’s not just the one mistake, it’s this feeling like I can’t totally trust myself with the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns outside of your work as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work? I don’t know. I’m trying to think.\n\nI mean, I definitely think about work stuff the most, because that’s where the mistakes are kind of… visible, you know? Like there’s a client or a deadline or other people see it. Outside of that, I’m not sure I’d say I repeatedly think about mistakes in the same way.\n\nLike, I’ll worry if I forgot something normal, I guess—sorry, I’m drifting—like I’ll leave a store and think, “Did I get everything?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the same loop as the work mistakes. So… I haven’t really noticed it as much outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns when it comes to your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, at least.\n\nLike with relationships, I’ll sometimes wonder if I said something awkward, but then I get distracted by—like, I was texting my sister the other day and then remembered I never paid that parking thing, and then I’m looking for the notice, and then I forgot what I was even worried about. Sorry, that’s not really answering it.\n\nDaily life stuff, I guess I have normal “did I forget something?” thoughts, but I don’t feel like I’m stuck replaying personal mistakes over and over the way I do with work. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas outside of work where you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can think of. I mean, I get bothered by random stuff, like if the apartment’s a mess or I forgot to call someone back, and then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m making coffee and then somehow I’m checking the weather and—sorry.\n\nBut repeatedly thinking about one thing outside of work? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the work mistakes do. It’s more like little things pop up and then I’m onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever bothered by thoughts about mistakes or concerns outside of work that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something I can’t stop thinking about. I mean, I’ll have little worries pop up, like “Did I forget to respond to that message?” or “Did I leave laundry in the washer?”—actually I do that a lot, leave it sitting there and then it smells weird, which is so annoying—but those thoughts don’t usually stick in my head for hours or anything.\n\nOutside of work it feels more scattered, I guess. Something bothers me, then something else grabs my attention, and I’m on to that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed being stuck on mistakes or concerns outside work in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are the thoughts about mistakes that bother you mostly about work, or do they also happen with things you do for fun or in your free time?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.\n\nThe thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m like, how did I miss that? I do this for a living.\n\nFor fun stuff… I don’t know, if I’m editing something personal or taking photos, I might get irritated if I do something sloppy, but it doesn’t hit the same. The work mistakes are the ones that stick because there are other people relying on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble stopping thoughts about mistakes when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say trouble stopping them, no. Like if I’m doing something I enjoy, I might notice I messed something up and get irritated for a minute—like, “ugh, that was dumb”—but then I usually end up distracted by the thing itself or some other detail.\n\nI mean, if I’m editing something just for me, I can get picky, but it doesn’t feel like those work mistakes where it keeps sitting in my chest afterward. With fun stuff it’s more like… annoyed, then I move on. Or I start fixing something else and forget what I was mad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thinking about mistakes at work is affecting your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I mean, I’m sure if I come home annoyed from work I’m probably not the most fun person to be around, but I can’t say, like, “yes, this is ruining my relationships” or anything.\n\nDaily life outside work… maybe it just follows me home a little sometimes? But then I’ll get caught up in dinner or a show or, like, realize I forgot to buy paper towels again and now that becomes the thing. So I’m not sure it’s the mistake-thinking specifically affecting everything outside work. I notice it most when I’m still in work mode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it bothers me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a lot of distress? It’s more like this uncomfortable buzzing in the background where I’m replaying it and getting mad at myself.\n\nBut then sometimes I’m also thinking about, like, whether I sent the invoice, or I need to charge batteries, or I forgot someone’s birthday—so it kind of gets mixed in with everything else. I know the work mistakes feel worse than other stuff, though. They make me feel embarrassed, I guess. Like I should be better than that.\n\nSo… distress, yes maybe, but I’m not sure how to measure “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain work tasks or responsibilities because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “I’m avoiding this because I might mess it up” kind of way.\n\nI mean, there are definitely tasks I’ll put off, but sometimes it’s just because they’re boring or I get pulled into something else, or someone asks me one quick thing and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a whole folder structure I didn’t need to touch. Which is not exactly the same thing, I guess.\n\nIf I’m worried about a mistake, I might double-check more or hover around it longer than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding responsibilities outright. At least I haven’t noticed myself doing that on purpose.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like I should be better than that.\n\nSo… distress, yes maybe, but I’m not sure how to measure “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain work tasks or responsibilities because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “I’m avoiding this because I might mess it up” kind of way.\n\nI mean, there are definitely tasks I’ll put off, but sometimes it’s just because they’re boring or I get pulled into something else, or someone asks me one quick thing and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a whole folder structure I didn’t need to touch. Which is not exactly the same thing, I guess.\n\nIf I’m worried about a mistake, I might double-check more or hover around it longer than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding responsibilities outright. At least I haven’t noticed myself doing that on purpose.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like thinking about these mistakes is interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or daily activities?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 38 / television_video_or_film_camera_operator_or_editor / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to watch something and my brain will randomly go, “Oh, remember that dumb thing you did earlier?” which is annoying.

But then again, I’m also the kind of person who pauses a movie five times because I remembered I needed to do laundry, or I start looking up an actor and somehow end up reading about where a camera lens was manufactured. So it’s hard for me to separate whether it’s the mistakes specifically ruining my free time, or just my brain not staying with the thing I’m doing.

I don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying stuff, exactly. It’s more like it can interrupt me, and then I get irritated for a bit, and then usually something else grabs my attention.

I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. Like there’s a client or a deadline or other people see it. It doesn’t feel like the same loop as the work mistakes. So… I haven’t really noticed it as much outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns when it comes to your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, at least.\n\nLike with relationships, I’ll sometimes wonder if I said something awkward, but then I get distracted by—like, I was texting my sister the other day and then remembered I never paid that parking thing, and then I’m looking for the notice, and then I forgot what I was even worried about. Sorry, that’s not really answering it.\n\nDaily life stuff, I guess I have normal “did I forget something?” thoughts, but I don’t feel like I’m stuck replaying personal mistakes over and over the way I do with work. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas outside of work where you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can think of. I mean, I get bothered by random stuff, like if the apartment’s a mess or I forgot to call someone back, and then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m making coffee and then somehow I’m checking the weather and—sorry.\n\nBut repeatedly thinking about one thing outside of work? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the work mistakes do. It’s more like little things pop up and then I’m onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever bothered by thoughts about mistakes or concerns outside of work that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something I can’t stop thinking about. I mean, I’ll have little worries pop up, like “Did I forget to respond to that message?” or “Did I leave laundry in the washer?”—actually I do that a lot, leave it sitting there and then it smells weird, which is so annoying—but those thoughts don’t usually stick in my head for hours or anything.\n\nOutside of work it feels more scattered, I guess. Something bothers me, then something else grabs my attention, and I’m on to that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed being stuck on mistakes or concerns outside work in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are the thoughts about mistakes that bother you mostly about work, or do they also happen with things you do for fun or in your free time?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.\n\nThe thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… probably more than I want to admit, but it’s not like I sit down for an hour to think about it on purpose.

It’s more that it keeps popping back in. Like, what finally scared me into coming in was I keep making these dumb technical mistakes I know better than to make—labeling camera cards wrong, missing a timecode note, exporting a client cut with the wrong lower-third even after I checked it. And then later I’ll be making dinner or walking somewhere and suddenly I’m replaying, “How did I miss that? I literally looked at it.”

So it takes up time in little chunks, I guess. Not always huge blocks, but enough that it feels like it’s following me around.

Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these mistakes later, are you only thinking about what happened, or are you also worried about making mistakes in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. And then the next project I’m worried I’m going to miss something like that again, so it’s not just the one mistake, it’s this feeling like I can’t totally trust myself with the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns outside of your work as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work? I don’t know. I’m trying to think.\n\nI mean, I definitely think about work stuff the most, because that’s where the mistakes are kind of… visible, you know? Like there’s a client or a deadline or other people see it. Outside of that, I’m not sure I’d say I repeatedly think about mistakes in the same way.\n\nLike, I’ll worry if I forgot something normal, I guess—sorry, I’m drifting—like I’ll leave a store and think, “Did I get everything?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the same loop as the work mistakes. So… I haven’t really noticed it as much outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns when it comes to your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, at least.\n\nLike with relationships, I’ll sometimes wonder if I said something awkward, but then I get distracted by—like, I was texting my sister the other day and then remembered I never paid that parking thing, and then I’m looking for the notice, and then I forgot what I was even worried about. Sorry, that’s not really answering it.\n\nDaily life stuff, I guess I have normal “did I forget something?” thoughts, but I don’t feel like I’m stuck replaying personal mistakes over and over the way I do with work. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas outside of work where you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can think of. I mean, I get bothered by random stuff, like if the apartment’s a mess or I forgot to call someone back, and then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m making coffee and then somehow I’m checking the weather and—sorry.\n\nBut repeatedly thinking about one thing outside of work? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the work mistakes do. It’s more like little things pop up and then I’m onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever bothered by thoughts about mistakes or concerns outside of work that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something I can’t stop thinking about. I mean, I’ll have little worries pop up, like “Did I forget to respond to that message?” or “Did I leave laundry in the washer?”—actually I do that a lot, leave it sitting there and then it smells weird, which is so annoying—but those thoughts don’t usually stick in my head for hours or anything.\n\nOutside of work it feels more scattered, I guess. Something bothers me, then something else grabs my attention, and I’m on to that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed being stuck on mistakes or concerns outside work in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are the thoughts about mistakes that bother you mostly about work, or do they also happen with things you do for fun or in your free time?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.\n\nThe thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m like, how did I miss that? I do this for a living.\n\nFor fun stuff… I don’t know, if I’m editing something personal or taking photos, I might get irritated if I do something sloppy, but it doesn’t hit the same. The work mistakes are the ones that stick because there are other people relying on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble stopping thoughts about mistakes when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say trouble stopping them, no. Like if I’m doing something I enjoy, I might notice I messed something up and get irritated for a minute—like, “ugh, that was dumb”—but then I usually end up distracted by the thing itself or some other detail.\n\nI mean, if I’m editing something just for me, I can get picky, but it doesn’t feel like those work mistakes where it keeps sitting in my chest afterward. With fun stuff it’s more like… annoyed, then I move on. Or I start fixing something else and forget what I was mad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thinking about mistakes at work is affecting your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I mean, I’m sure if I come home annoyed from work I’m probably not the most fun person to be around, but I can’t say, like, “yes, this is ruining my relationships” or anything.\n\nDaily life outside work… maybe it just follows me home a little sometimes? But then I’ll get caught up in dinner or a show or, like, realize I forgot to buy paper towels again and now that becomes the thing. So I’m not sure it’s the mistake-thinking specifically affecting everything outside work. I notice it most when I’m still in work mode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it bothers me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a lot of distress? It’s more like this uncomfortable buzzing in the background where I’m replaying it and getting mad at myself.\n\nBut then sometimes I’m also thinking about, like, whether I sent the invoice, or I need to charge batteries, or I forgot someone’s birthday—so it kind of gets mixed in with everything else. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas outside of work where you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can think of. I mean, I get bothered by random stuff, like if the apartment’s a mess or I forgot to call someone back, and then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m making coffee and then somehow I’m checking the weather and—sorry.\n\nBut repeatedly thinking about one thing outside of work? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the work mistakes do. It’s more like little things pop up and then I’m onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever bothered by thoughts about mistakes or concerns outside of work that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something I can’t stop thinking about. I mean, I’ll have little worries pop up, like “Did I forget to respond to that message?” or “Did I leave laundry in the washer?”—actually I do that a lot, leave it sitting there and then it smells weird, which is so annoying—but those thoughts don’t usually stick in my head for hours or anything.\n\nOutside of work it feels more scattered, I guess. Something bothers me, then something else grabs my attention, and I’m on to that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed being stuck on mistakes or concerns outside work in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are the thoughts about mistakes that bother you mostly about work, or do they also happen with things you do for fun or in your free time?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.\n\nThe thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m like, how did I miss that? I do this for a living.\n\nFor fun stuff… I don’t know, if I’m editing something personal or taking photos, I might get irritated if I do something sloppy, but it doesn’t hit the same. The work mistakes are the ones that stick because there are other people relying on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble stopping thoughts about mistakes when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say trouble stopping them, no. Like if I’m doing something I enjoy, I might notice I messed something up and get irritated for a minute—like, “ugh, that was dumb”—but then I usually end up distracted by the thing itself or some other detail.\n\nI mean, if I’m editing something just for me, I can get picky, but it doesn’t feel like those work mistakes where it keeps sitting in my chest afterward. With fun stuff it’s more like… annoyed, then I move on. Or I start fixing something else and forget what I was mad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thinking about mistakes at work is affecting your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I mean, I’m sure if I come home annoyed from work I’m probably not the most fun person to be around, but I can’t say, like, “yes, this is ruining my relationships” or anything.\n\nDaily life outside work… maybe it just follows me home a little sometimes? But then I’ll get caught up in dinner or a show or, like, realize I forgot to buy paper towels again and now that becomes the thing. So I’m not sure it’s the mistake-thinking specifically affecting everything outside work. I notice it most when I’m still in work mode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it bothers me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a lot of distress? It’s more like this uncomfortable buzzing in the background where I’m replaying it and getting mad at myself.\n\nBut then sometimes I’m also thinking about, like, whether I sent the invoice, or I need to charge batteries, or I forgot someone’s birthday—so it kind of gets mixed in with everything else. I know the work mistakes feel worse than other stuff, though. They make me feel embarrassed, I guess. Like I should be better than that.\n\nSo… distress, yes maybe, but I’m not sure how to measure “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain work tasks or responsibilities because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “I’m avoiding this because I might mess it up” kind of way.\n\nI mean, there are definitely tasks I’ll put off, but sometimes it’s just because they’re boring or I get pulled into something else, or someone asks me one quick thing and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a whole folder structure I didn’t need to touch. Which is not exactly the same thing, I guess.\n\nIf I’m worried about a mistake, I might double-check more or hover around it longer than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding responsibilities outright. At least I haven’t noticed myself doing that on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to watch something and my brain will randomly go, “Oh, remember that dumb thing you did earlier?” which is annoying.\n\nBut then again, I’m also the kind of person who pauses a movie five times because I remembered I needed to do laundry, or I start looking up an actor and somehow end up reading about where a camera lens was manufactured. So it’s hard for me to separate whether it’s the mistakes specifically ruining my free time, or just my brain not staying with the thing I’m doing.\n\nI don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying stuff, exactly. It’s more like it can interrupt me, and then I get irritated for a bit, and then usually something else grabs my attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that thinking about these mistakes takes up a significant amount of your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably more than I want to admit, but it’s not like I sit down for an hour to think about it on purpose.\n\nIt’s more that it keeps popping back in. Like, what finally scared me into coming in was I keep making these dumb technical mistakes I know better than to make—labeling camera cards wrong, missing a timecode note, exporting a client cut with the wrong lower-third even after I checked it. And then later I’ll be making dinner or walking somewhere and suddenly I’m replaying, “How did I miss that? I literally looked at it.”\n\nSo it takes up time in little chunks, I guess. Not always huge blocks, but enough that it feels like it’s following me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes at work or your thinking about them is causing problems in your relationships with coworkers or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… with coworkers, yes, I think so. Not like people are mad at me socially, exactly, but there’s been a shift.\n\nLike, my producers used to just hand me the complicated shoots and trust I’d catch everything. And my camera work is still good — I know it is — but because of these stupid mistakes around the details, I can tell they’re checking behind me more, or giving the more complex stuff to someone else.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think it is. I mean, I’m still doing good camera work — that’s the annoying part. Like, when I’m actually behind the camera, I know what I’m doing.

But my producers have definitely started trusting me less with the more complicated shoots. And it’s not even always said directly, it’s just… suddenly someone else is getting handed the stuff I would’ve gotten before, or there’s more checking behind me.

And I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, so it feels like that’s slipping. Which makes me spiral a little, because I’ll be like, “Okay, is this one mislabeled card really the thing that makes people stop seeing me as capable?” But then, you know, in production, one small detail can become a whole stupid domino effect. So… yeah, career-wise, it feels significant.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42, F43.0, F48.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah—so, what finally scared me into making the appointment is work stuff. I’m a camera operator/editor, and I keep making these really stupid technical mistakes that I absolutely know better than to make.\n\nLike I’ll label camera cards wrong, or miss a timecode note, or export a client cut with the wrong lower-third still in it even after I swear I checked it. And the thing is, these aren’t complicated things—like, I train other people on this kind of workflow sometimes. It’s embarrassing. I’ll be driving home and suddenly think, “Wait, did I fix that graphic?” and then it ruins my whole evening. So… yeah, that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about these mistakes after they happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, it’s not like I’m trying to sit there and obsess over it, but those mistakes stick in my head because they’re so… basic. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or sending out an export and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I thought I checked it.\n\nAnd then I’ll replay it, like, “How did I miss that?” It’s embarrassing because I know that workflow. I’ve done it a million times. Sometimes I’ll be thinking about it later when I’m doing something totally unrelated, like making dinner, and then I’m annoyed all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something to try to prevent these mistakes from happening again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I try to. That’s kind of the whole problem—I’ll make these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make, like labeling camera cards wrong or missing a timecode note, and then I’ll think, “Okay, I need a better system.”\n\nSo I’ll check the export again, or look at the lower-thirds again, because I’ve sent out a client cut with the wrong one still in it even after I thought I checked. Which is… ugh, it’s embarrassing. And then I start making little notes to myself, or trying to be extra careful, but sometimes I get sidetracked by another part of the workflow. Like someone asks me about a shot list or whatever, and suddenly I’m not even sure if I actually checked the thing or just thought about checking it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform certain actions in a specific way to feel right or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure. Not in a “specific way” exactly? Like, I don’t have a ritual or anything where I have to do it three times or it feels wrong.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll try to check stuff because I don’t trust that I caught it the first time, but then—sorry, this is probably not what you asked—I’ll get pulled into some other detail, like whether the audio levels were weird or if the client wanted the blue version of the graphic, and then I’m halfway through thinking about that instead.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there’s a particular action I have to do in a certain way to prevent something bad. It’s more messy than that.\n\nDoctor: When you're thinking about these mistakes later, are you only thinking about what happened, or are you also worried about making mistakes in the future?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I think. Like at first I’m replaying what actually happened—“How did I label that camera card wrong?” or “Why did I miss that timecode note?”—but then it turns into, “What if I do it again?”\n\nThat’s honestly what scared me into coming in. I keep making these dumb technical mistakes that I know better than to make. Like exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was wrong even though I had already checked it, or thought I checked it. And then the next project I’m worried I’m going to miss something like that again, so it’s not just the one mistake, it’s this feeling like I can’t totally trust myself with the details.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns outside of your work as well?\n\nPatient: Um… outside of work? I don’t know. I’m trying to think.\n\nI mean, I definitely think about work stuff the most, because that’s where the mistakes are kind of… visible, you know? Like there’s a client or a deadline or other people see it. Outside of that, I’m not sure I’d say I repeatedly think about mistakes in the same way.\n\nLike, I’ll worry if I forgot something normal, I guess—sorry, I’m drifting—like I’ll leave a store and think, “Did I get everything?” but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the same loop as the work mistakes. So… I haven’t really noticed it as much outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly thinking about mistakes or concerns when it comes to your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the same way, at least.\n\nLike with relationships, I’ll sometimes wonder if I said something awkward, but then I get distracted by—like, I was texting my sister the other day and then remembered I never paid that parking thing, and then I’m looking for the notice, and then I forgot what I was even worried about. Sorry, that’s not really answering it.\n\nDaily life stuff, I guess I have normal “did I forget something?” thoughts, but I don’t feel like I’m stuck replaying personal mistakes over and over the way I do with work. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas outside of work where you find yourself repeatedly thinking about something that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: Not really that I can think of. I mean, I get bothered by random stuff, like if the apartment’s a mess or I forgot to call someone back, and then I’ll be thinking about that while I’m making coffee and then somehow I’m checking the weather and—sorry.\n\nBut repeatedly thinking about one thing outside of work? I don’t know. Nothing is coming to mind the way the work mistakes do. It’s more like little things pop up and then I’m onto something else.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever bothered by thoughts about mistakes or concerns outside of work that you can't seem to stop thinking about?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like something I can’t stop thinking about. I mean, I’ll have little worries pop up, like “Did I forget to respond to that message?” or “Did I leave laundry in the washer?”—actually I do that a lot, leave it sitting there and then it smells weird, which is so annoying—but those thoughts don’t usually stick in my head for hours or anything.\n\nOutside of work it feels more scattered, I guess. Something bothers me, then something else grabs my attention, and I’m on to that. So… no, I haven’t really noticed being stuck on mistakes or concerns outside work in that way.\n\nDoctor: Are the thoughts about mistakes that bother you mostly about work, or do they also happen with things you do for fun or in your free time?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I think. Like, if I mess up something in my free time, I’m annoyed, but I don’t spiral on it the same way.\n\nThe thing that really scared me into coming in was work stuff—technical things I absolutely know better than to mess up. Like labeling camera cards wrong, or missing a timecode note, or exporting a client cut and then realizing the lower-third was the wrong one even though I swear I checked it. And then I’m like, how did I miss that? I do this for a living.\n\nFor fun stuff… I don’t know, if I’m editing something personal or taking photos, I might get irritated if I do something sloppy, but it doesn’t hit the same. The work mistakes are the ones that stick because there are other people relying on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have trouble stopping thoughts about mistakes when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say trouble stopping them, no. Like if I’m doing something I enjoy, I might notice I messed something up and get irritated for a minute—like, “ugh, that was dumb”—but then I usually end up distracted by the thing itself or some other detail.\n\nI mean, if I’m editing something just for me, I can get picky, but it doesn’t feel like those work mistakes where it keeps sitting in my chest afterward. With fun stuff it’s more like… annoyed, then I move on. Or I start fixing something else and forget what I was mad about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thinking about mistakes at work is affecting your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really thought of it that way. I mean, I’m sure if I come home annoyed from work I’m probably not the most fun person to be around, but I can’t say, like, “yes, this is ruining my relationships” or anything.\n\nDaily life outside work… maybe it just follows me home a little sometimes? But then I’ll get caught up in dinner or a show or, like, realize I forgot to buy paper towels again and now that becomes the thing. So I’m not sure it’s the mistake-thinking specifically affecting everything outside work. I notice it most when I’m still in work mode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it bothers me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a lot of distress? It’s more like this uncomfortable buzzing in the background where I’m replaying it and getting mad at myself.\n\nBut then sometimes I’m also thinking about, like, whether I sent the invoice, or I need to charge batteries, or I forgot someone’s birthday—so it kind of gets mixed in with everything else. I know the work mistakes feel worse than other stuff, though. They make me feel embarrassed, I guess. Like I should be better than that.\n\nSo… distress, yes maybe, but I’m not sure how to measure “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain work tasks or responsibilities because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “I’m avoiding this because I might mess it up” kind of way.\n\nI mean, there are definitely tasks I’ll put off, but sometimes it’s just because they’re boring or I get pulled into something else, or someone asks me one quick thing and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a whole folder structure I didn’t need to touch. Which is not exactly the same thing, I guess.\n\nIf I’m worried about a mistake, I might double-check more or hover around it longer than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding responsibilities outright. At least I haven’t noticed myself doing that on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to watch something and my brain will randomly go, “Oh, remember that dumb thing you did earlier?” which is annoying.\n\nBut then again, I’m also the kind of person who pauses a movie five times because I remembered I needed to do laundry, or I start looking up an actor and somehow end up reading about where a camera lens was manufactured. So it’s hard for me to separate whether it’s the mistakes specifically ruining my free time, or just my brain not staying with the thing I’m doing.\n\nI don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying stuff, exactly. It’s more like it can interrupt me, and then I get irritated for a bit, and then usually something else grabs my attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that thinking about these mistakes takes up a significant amount of your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably more than I want to admit, but it’s not like I sit down for an hour to think about it on purpose.\n\nIt’s more that it keeps popping back in. Like, what finally scared me into coming in was I keep making these dumb technical mistakes I know better than to make—labeling camera cards wrong, missing a timecode note, exporting a client cut with the wrong lower-third even after I checked it. And then later I’ll be making dinner or walking somewhere and suddenly I’m replaying, “How did I miss that? I literally looked at it.”\n\nSo it takes up time in little chunks, I guess. Not always huge blocks, but enough that it feels like it’s following me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes at work or your thinking about them is causing problems in your relationships with coworkers or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… with coworkers, yes, I think so. Not like people are mad at me socially, exactly, but there’s been a shift.\n\nLike, my producers used to just hand me the complicated shoots and trust I’d catch everything. And my camera work is still good — I know it is — but because of these stupid mistakes around the details, I can tell they’re checking behind me more, or giving the more complex stuff to someone else.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away. Which is frustrating, because it’s not that I can’t do the creative or technical work. It’s more like these little errors are making people trust me less.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mistakes at work or thinking about them is significantly impacting your ability to perform your job or advance in your career?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, I’m still doing good camera work — that’s the annoying part. Like, when I’m actually behind the camera, I know what I’m doing.\n\nBut my producers have definitely started trusting me less with the more complicated shoots. Which makes me spiral a little, because I’ll be like, “Okay, is this one mislabeled card really the thing that makes people stop seeing me as capable?” But then, you know, in production, one small detail can become a whole stupid domino effect. I mean, I’m sure if I come home annoyed from work I’m probably not the most fun person to be around, but I can’t say, like, “yes, this is ruining my relationships” or anything.\n\nDaily life outside work… maybe it just follows me home a little sometimes? But then I’ll get caught up in dinner or a show or, like, realize I forgot to buy paper towels again and now that becomes the thing. So I’m not sure it’s the mistake-thinking specifically affecting everything outside work. I notice it most when I’m still in work mode, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it bothers me, but I don’t know if I’d call it a lot of distress? It’s more like this uncomfortable buzzing in the background where I’m replaying it and getting mad at myself.\n\nBut then sometimes I’m also thinking about, like, whether I sent the invoice, or I need to charge batteries, or I forgot someone’s birthday—so it kind of gets mixed in with everything else. I know the work mistakes feel worse than other stuff, though. They make me feel embarrassed, I guess. Like I should be better than that.\n\nSo… distress, yes maybe, but I’m not sure how to measure “a lot.”\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain work tasks or responsibilities because you're worried about making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in a clear “I’m avoiding this because I might mess it up” kind of way.\n\nI mean, there are definitely tasks I’ll put off, but sometimes it’s just because they’re boring or I get pulled into something else, or someone asks me one quick thing and then suddenly I’m reorganizing a whole folder structure I didn’t need to touch. Which is not exactly the same thing, I guess.\n\nIf I’m worried about a mistake, I might double-check more or hover around it longer than I need to, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding responsibilities outright. At least I haven’t noticed myself doing that on purpose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like thinking about these mistakes is interfering with your ability to enjoy your free time or daily activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll be sitting there trying to watch something and my brain will randomly go, “Oh, remember that dumb thing you did earlier?” which is annoying.\n\nBut then again, I’m also the kind of person who pauses a movie five times because I remembered I needed to do laundry, or I start looking up an actor and somehow end up reading about where a camera lens was manufactured. So it’s hard for me to separate whether it’s the mistakes specifically ruining my free time, or just my brain not staying with the thing I’m doing.\n\nI don’t think I’ve stopped enjoying stuff, exactly. It’s more like it can interrupt me, and then I get irritated for a bit, and then usually something else grabs my attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that thinking about these mistakes takes up a significant amount of your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably more than I want to admit, but it’s not like I sit down for an hour to think about it on purpose.\n\nIt’s more that it keeps popping back in. Like, what finally scared me into coming in was I keep making these dumb technical mistakes I know better than to make—labeling camera cards wrong, missing a timecode note, exporting a client cut with the wrong lower-third even after I checked it. And then later I’ll be making dinner or walking somewhere and suddenly I’m replaying, “How did I miss that? I literally looked at it.”\n\nSo it takes up time in little chunks, I guess. Not always huge blocks, but enough that it feels like it’s following me around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your mistakes at work or your thinking about them is causing problems in your relationships with coworkers or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… with coworkers, yes, I think so. Not like people are mad at me socially, exactly, but there’s been a shift.\n\nLike, my producers used to just hand me the complicated shoots and trust I’d catch everything. And my camera work is still good — I know it is — but because of these stupid mistakes around the details, I can tell they’re checking behind me more, or giving the more complex stuff to someone else.\n\nAnd that’s the part that really bothers me, because I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, and it feels like that’s slipping away. Which is frustrating, because it’s not that I can’t do the creative or technical work. It’s more like these little errors are making people trust me less.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your mistakes at work or thinking about them is significantly impacting your ability to perform your job or advance in your career?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it is. I mean, I’m still doing good camera work — that’s the annoying part. Like, when I’m actually behind the camera, I know what I’m doing.\n\nBut my producers have definitely started trusting me less with the more complicated shoots. And it’s not even always said directly, it’s just… suddenly someone else is getting handed the stuff I would’ve gotten before, or there’s more checking behind me.\n\nAnd I’ve been trying to move toward a senior director of photography role, so it feels like that’s slipping. Which makes me spiral a little, because I’ll be like, “Okay, is this one mislabeled card really the thing that makes people stop seeing me as capable?” But then, you know, in production, one small detail can become a whole stupid domino effect.
